Appendix A
Derivation of the Lindblad-Keldysh action

Here we provide a detailed derivation of the Keldysh partition function for a
driven open system, focusing on the example of a single bosonic or fermionic
field mode
\hat{\psi}
. We pay special attention to the subtle issue of
operator ordering. As we argue in the following and have anticipated in
Eq. (
7
), the proper way to keep track of
operator ordering is to introduce a temporal regularization of the Keldysh
action in the form of infinitesimal time shifts
Sieberer et al. 2014
. The origin
of the time shifts is understood most transparently by performing the steps that
lead to the master equation (
1
), starting from a Hamiltonian
system-bath setting, in the Keldysh formalism
Sieberer et al. 2014
. In the
following, we first briefly summarize this derivation and generalize it to
fermionic fields. Then, we present a derivation of the Keldysh partition
function starting directly from the master equation (
1
).

A.1
Derivation from a system-bath setting

The Hamiltonian that describes the dynamics of a system coupled to a bath is of
the form
\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{s}+\hat{H}_{sb}+\hat{H}_{b}
. We assume that the
system Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{s}
is explicitly time-dependent due to a classical
driving field with frequency
\omega_{0}
, but becomes time-independent after a
transformation to a suitably chosen rotating frame, generated by an operator

\hat{H}_{0}
. A paradigmatic example is given by a two-level system undergoing
Rabi oscillations. The system-bath coupling
\hat{H}_{sb}
and the bath
Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{b}
are given in Eq. (
17
), where the bath
modes
\hat{\phi}_{\mu}
with frequencies
\omega_{\mu}
are bosonic when the
system operator
\hat{L}
in
\hat{H}_{sb}
is bosonic or even in fermionic
operators; in contrast, when
\hat{L}
is odd in fermionic operators, the bath
modes are fermionic.

We perform a transformation to a rotating frame described by the time-dependent
unitary operator
\hat{U}=e^{i\left(\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{b}\right)t}
.
By assumption, the system Hamiltonian becomes time-independent in the rotating
frame; the bath Hamiltonian is removed by the transformation, and the
system-bath coupling becomes

\hat{H}_{sb}^{\prime}=\sum_{\mu}g_{\mu}\left(\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{\phi}_{\mu}e^{-i\left(\omega_{\mu}-\omega_{0}\right)t}+\hat{\phi}_{\mu}^{\dagger}\hat{L}e^{i\left(\omega_{\mu}-\omega_{0}\right)t}\right).

(119)

We note that a more general form of the system-bath coupling contains also the
terms
\hat{L}\hat{\phi}_{\mu}
and

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{\phi}_{\mu}^{\dagger}
. In the rotating frame, such
terms oscillate with frequency
\omega_{\mu}+\omega_{0}
, and are dropped in a
rotating wave approximation.

After bringing the system Hamiltonian as well as
\hat{L}
and

\hat{L}^{\dagger}
to normal ordered form, we can construct the usual Keldysh
functional integral for the system-bath setup without
ambiguity
Altland and Simons 2010
;
Kamenev 2023
. We introduce an additional sign

\zeta=-1
for fermionic fields on the backward branch. This sign results
from the representation of a trace in fermionic Fock space in the basis of
coherent states, and allows us to obtain a Keldysh action that satisfies the
usual causality structure described for the action in
Sec.
II.2.2
and for the Green’s functions in
Appendix
B
. Integrating out the bath yields the
following contribution to the Keldysh action:

\Delta S=-\sum_{\mu}g_{\mu}^{2}\int_{t,t^{\prime}}\left(L^{\dagger}_{+}(t),\zeta_{b}L^{\dagger}_{-}(t)\right)e^{i\omega_{0}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)}\\
\times\hat{\sigma}_{z}\begin{pmatrix}G_{\mu}^{++}(t-t^{\prime})&G_{\mu}^{+-}(t-t^{\prime})\\
G_{\mu}^{-+}(t-t^{\prime})&G_{\mu}^{--}(t-t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\hat{\sigma}_{z}\begin{pmatrix}L_{+}(t^{\prime})\\
\zeta_{b}L_{-}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix},

(120)

where the Green’s functions of the bath are given by

\begin{split}G_{\mu}^{+-}(t)&=-i\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{\mu})e^{-i\omega_{\mu}t},\\
G_{\mu}^{-+}(t)&=-i\left(1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{\mu})\right)e^{-i\omega_{\mu}t},\\
G_{\mu}^{++}(t)&=\theta(-t)G_{\mu}^{+-}(t)+\theta(t)G_{\mu}^{-+}(t),\\
G_{\mu}^{--}(t)&=\theta(t)G_{\mu}^{+-}(t)+\theta(-t)G_{\mu}^{-+}(t).\end{split}

(121)

In the above equations, the sign
\zeta_{b}=\pm 1
refers to the bath:

\zeta_{b}=+1
for a bosonic bath, and
\zeta_{b}=-1
for a fermionic
bath. Accordingly, the distribution function is

n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega)=\left.1\middle/\left(e^{\beta\omega}-\zeta_{b}\right)\right.
.
A possible additional sign is contained in the field variables
L_{\pm}
and

L_{\pm}^{\dagger}
. For example, for
\hat{L}=\hat{\psi}
, we obtain

L_{+}=\psi_{+}
,
L_{-}=\zeta\psi_{-}
, and
L^{\dagger}_{+}=\psi_{+}^{*}
,

L^{\dagger}_{-}=\zeta\psi_{-}^{*}
.

We assume that the bath remains in thermal equilibrium at all times—this is
essentially the Born approximation, and relies on weak system-bath
coupling. Further, we wish to perform the Markov approximation, assuming that
the correlation time of the bath is short on typical time scales of evolution of
the system, such that we can replace
L_{\pm}(t^{\prime})\to L_{\pm}(t)
in
Eq. (
120
). However, we have to keep track of the temporal order of

L_{\pm}^{\dagger}(t)
and
L_{\pm}(t^{\prime})
that is encoded in the Heaviside
functions in Eq. (
121
) as can be seen by introducing a new
integration variable
\tau=t-t^{\prime}
and rewriting Eq. (
120
) as

\Delta S=-\int_{t,\tau}\left[L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)\left(\theta(-\tau)\Gamma^{+}(\tau)+\theta(\tau)\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\right)L_{+}(t-\tau)\right.\\
-L_{-}(t-\tau)\Gamma^{+}(\tau)L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)-L_{+}(t-\tau)\Gamma^{-}(\tau)L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)\\
\left.+L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)\left(\theta(\tau)\Gamma^{+}(\tau)+\theta(-\tau)\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\right)L_{-}(t-\tau)\right],

(122)

where

\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma^{+}(\tau)\\
\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{\mu}g_{\mu}^{2}e^{i\omega_{0}\tau}\begin{pmatrix}G_{\mu}^{+-}(\tau)\\
G_{\mu}^{-+}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}.

(123)

In the terms that are mixed in the branch index, we have exchanged the order of

L_{\pm}^{\dagger}(t)
and
L_{\pm}(t-\tau)
. If the jump operator is odd in
fermionic operators, this leads to an additional minus sign, which cancels

\zeta_{b}
in Eq. (
120
).

Next, we consider the continuum limit of dense bath frequencies, centered around

\omega_{b}\approx\omega_{0}
with a large bandwidth
\vartheta
. We can then
replace the sum over bath modes
\mu
by an integral,

\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma^{+}(\tau)\\
\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}=-i\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\,\nu(\omega)g(\omega)^{2}e^{i\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)\tau}\begin{pmatrix}\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega)\\
1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}.

(124)

where
\nu(\omega)
is the density of states of the bath. The key assumption
underlying the Markov approximation is that the product

\nu(\omega)g(\omega)^{2}
is a smoothly varying function of
\omega
in the
vicinity of
\omega_{0}
. This implies that the bath correlation functions

\Gamma^{\pm}(\tau)
appearing in Eq. (
122
) are sharply peaked
around
\tau=0
. Therefore, keeping track of temporal order, we make the
replacement

\theta(\pm\tau)L_{\pm}(t-\tau)\to\theta(\pm\tau)L_{\pm}(t_{\mp})
where

t_{\pm}\to t\pm 0^{+}
. We can now perform the integration over
\tau
using

\begin{split}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\tau\,e^{i\omega\tau}\theta(\pm\tau)=\pi\delta(\omega)\pm i\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega},\end{split}

(125)

where
\mathcal{P}
denotes the principal value. This leads to

\begin{split}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\tau\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma^{+}(\tau)\\
\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}&=-i\gamma\begin{pmatrix}\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\\
1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\end{pmatrix},\\
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\tau\,\theta(\pm\tau)\begin{pmatrix}\Gamma^{+}(\tau)\\
\Gamma^{-}(\tau)\end{pmatrix}&=-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\\
1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\end{pmatrix}\mp\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{+}(\omega_{0})\\
\Delta_{-}(\omega_{0})\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

(126)

where
\gamma=2\pi\nu(\omega_{0})g(\omega_{0})^{2}
, and

\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{+}(\omega_{0})\\
\Delta_{-}(\omega_{0})\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{P}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\omega\,\frac{\nu(\omega)g(\omega)^{2}}{\omega_{0}-\omega}\begin{pmatrix}\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega)\\
1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}.

(127)

We thus find that
\Delta S=\Delta S_{d}+\Delta S_{L}
is the sum of a
dissipative contribution and a Lamb shift, which are given by

\Delta S_{d}=-i\gamma\int_{t}\left\{\left(1+\zeta_{b}n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\right)\vphantom{\frac{1}{2}}\right.\\
\times\left[L_{+}(t)L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)L_{+}(t_{-})+L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)L_{-}(t_{+})\right)\right]\\
\left.+n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})\left[L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)L_{-}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{+}(t_{+})L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)+L_{-}(t_{-})L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)\right)\right]\right\},

(128)

and

\Delta S_{L}=-\int_{t}\left[\Delta_{+}(\omega_{0})\left(L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)L_{+}(t_{+})-L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)L_{-}(t_{-})\right)\right.\\
\left.-\Delta_{-}(\omega_{0})\left(L_{+}^{\dagger}(t)L_{+}(t_{-})-L_{-}^{\dagger}(t)L_{-}(t_{+})\right)\right].

(129)

For a bath at zero temperature, we can set
n_{\zeta_{b}}(\omega_{0})=0
. Then,
after absorbing
\sqrt{\gamma}
in a redefinition of
L_{\pm}
,
Eq. (
128
) yields Eq. (
7
). The
Lamb shift can be included in the Hamiltonian contribution to the action.

A.2
Derivation from the quantum master equation

The above derivation of the Keldysh action for a driven open system shows that
infinitesimal time shifts occur naturally in a Hamiltonian system-bath
setting. Moreover, above we have required normal order only of
\hat{H}
,

\hat{L}
, and
\hat{L}^{\dagger}
, but not of the product

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
that also appears in the master
equation (
1
). These insights serve as a guideline for a direct
derivation of the Keldysh action from the master equation
Sieberer et al. 2016a
,
which we generalize here to fermionic systems.

A key ingredient in the construction of the functional integral representation
are coherent states. For bosons, a coherent state with amplitude
\psi\in\mathbb{C}

is defined as
\lvert\psi\rangle=e^{\psi\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}}\lvert 0\rangle
, where the
vacuum state
\lvert 0\rangle
obeys
\hat{\psi}\lvert 0\rangle=0
. Defining fermionic
coherent states, on the other hand, requires us to employ Grassmann numbers that
form an anticommutative algebra. To unify the presentation for bosons and
fermions, we will denote also Grassmann numbers with
\psi
. Then, a fermionic
coherent state is given by

\lvert\psi\rangle=e^{-\psi\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}}\lvert 0\rangle
. Both bosonic and
fermionic coherent states have the following properties: They are eigenstates of
the annihilation operator,
\hat{\psi}\lvert\psi\rangle=\psi\lvert\psi\rangle
; the
overlap of two coherent states is
\langle\psi|\psi^{\prime}\rangle=e^{\psi^{*}\psi^{\prime}}
;
and coherent states span the bosonic or fermionic Fock space, as expressed by
the completeness relation

\hat{1}=\int\frac{d\psi^{*}d\psi}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\,e^{-\psi^{*}\psi}\lvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvert.

(130)

However, there is a fundamental difference between bosons and fermions: For
bosons,
\psi^{*}
denotes the complex conjugate of
\psi\in\mathbb{C}
. In contrast,
the notation
\psi^{*}
for a Grassmann number
\psi
is merely
symbolic. Indeed, for fermions,
\psi
and
\psi^{*}
are strictly independent
variables
Wegner 2016
.

As anticipated in Sec.
II.2
, the starting point for
the construction of the functional integral representation is the formal
solution of the master equation,

\hat{\rho}(t)=e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})
. We
split the evolution from
t_{0}
to
t
into
N
discrete steps of duration

\Delta t=\left(t-t_{0}\right)/N
,

\hat{\rho}(t)=e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})=\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\Delta t}\right)^{N}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\approx\left(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{L}\Delta t\right)^{N}\hat{\rho}(t_{0}),

(131)

where
\mathcal{I}
is the identity superoperator. We denote the density matrix
at time
t_{n}=t_{0}+n\Delta t
by
\hat{\rho}_{n}=\hat{\rho}(t_{n})
. The
evolution during one time step is thus given by

\hat{\rho}_{n+1}\approx\left(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{L}\Delta t\right)\hat{\rho}_{n}.

(132)

To keep track of the order of operators, we further split each time step into
two, leading to a total of
2N
time steps. Details of this splitting are given
below. We construct a field integral representation of the time evolution by
inserting resolutions of the identity in terms of coherent states
Eq. (
130
) in between consecutive time steps and
both to the left and to the right of the density matrix. In particular, for the
density matrix
\hat{\rho}_{n}
at time
t_{n}
, which is reached after
2n
time
steps, we write

\hat{\rho}_{n}=\int\frac{d\psi_{+,2n}^{*}d\psi_{+,2n}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\frac{d\psi_{-,2n}^{*}d\psi_{-,2n}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}e^{-\psi_{+,2n}^{*}\psi_{+,2n}-\psi_{-,2n}^{*}\psi_{-,2n}}\\
\times\langle\psi_{+,2n}|\hat{\rho}_{n}|\zeta\psi_{-,2n}\rangle\lvert\psi_{+,2n}\rangle\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2n}\rvert.

(133)

As explained in Sec.
A.1
, we have to
include a sign
\zeta=-1
for Grassmann variables on the backward
branch.

To obtain a representation of the full time evolution, we have to relate the
matrix element

\langle\psi_{+,2n+2}|\hat{\rho}_{n+1}|\zeta\psi_{-,2n+2}\rangle
,
appearing in the coherent state representation of
\hat{\rho}_{n+1}
, to the
corresponding matrix element of
\hat{\rho}_{n}
. This can be done by inserting
Eq. (
133
) in
Eq. (
132
), leading us to consider the matrix element of the
superoperator
\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{L}\Delta t
appearing in
Eq. (
132
),

\langle\psi_{+,2n+2}|\left(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{L}\Delta t\right)\!\left(\lvert\psi_{+,2n}\rangle\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2n}\rvert\right)|\zeta\psi_{-,2n+2}\rangle
,
which contains, in particular, the terms

\langle\psi_{+,2n+2}|\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}|\psi_{+,2n}\rangle
and

\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2n}|\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}|\zeta\psi_{-,2n+2}\rangle
.
When
\hat{L}
and
\hat{L}^{\dagger}
are normal ordered, their product

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
is in general not normal ordered. Therefore, we
insert additional resolutions of the identity in terms of

\lvert\psi_{+,2n+1}\rangle
and
\lvert\zeta\psi_{-,2n+1}\rangle
between

\hat{L}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{L}
. In other matrix elements such as

\langle\psi_{+,2n+2}|\hat{H}|\psi_{+,2n}\rangle
we also insert these
additional resolutions of the identity. The position, e.g., before or after

\hat{H}
, is arbitrary, and we make the choice of applying the Hamiltonian

\hat{H}
and the jump operator
\hat{L}
on the forward and
\hat{L}^{\dagger}

on backward branch between steps
2n
and
2n+1
. We can then replace the
matrix elements of operators by functions of the coherent state amplitudes, both
for the Hamiltonian,

H(\psi^{*},\psi^{\prime})=\langle\psi|\hat{H}|\psi^{\prime}\rangle/\langle\psi|\psi^{\prime}\rangle
,
and the jump operator,

L(\psi^{*},\psi^{\prime})=\langle\psi|\hat{L}|\psi^{\prime}\rangle/\langle\psi|\psi^{\prime}\rangle
,
and its Hermitian conjugate,

L^{\dagger}(\psi^{*},\psi^{\prime})=\langle\psi|\hat{L}^{\dagger}|\psi^{\prime}\rangle/\langle\psi|\psi^{\prime}\rangle
.
Specifically, following our convention for inserting resolutions of the
identity, we obtain the following matrix elements:

\displaystyle H_{+,n}

\displaystyle=H(\psi_{+,2n+1}^{*},\psi_{+,2n}),

\displaystyle H_{-,n}

\displaystyle=H(\zeta\psi_{-,2n}^{*},\zeta\psi_{-,2n+1}),

(134)

\displaystyle L_{+,n}

\displaystyle=L(\psi_{+,2n+1}^{*},\psi_{+,2n}),

\displaystyle L_{-,n}

\displaystyle=L(\zeta\psi_{-,2n+1}^{*},\zeta\psi_{-,2n+2}),

\displaystyle L^{\dagger}_{+,n}

\displaystyle=L^{\dagger}(\psi_{+,2n+2}^{*},\psi_{+,2n+1}),

\displaystyle L^{\dagger}_{-,n}

\displaystyle=L^{\dagger}(\zeta\psi_{-,2n}^{*},\zeta\psi_{-,2n+1}),

in terms of which we can write the Lindbladian as

\mathcal{L}_{+-,n}=-i\left(H_{+,n}-H_{-,n}\right)\\
+L_{+,n}L^{\dagger}_{-,n}-\frac{1}{2}\left(L^{\dagger}_{+,n}L_{+,n}+L^{\dagger}_{-,n}L_{-,n}\right).

(135)

We thus find the desired relation between the matrix elements of the density
matrix at times
t_{n+1}
and
t_{n}
:

\langle\psi_{+,2n+2}|\hat{\rho}_{n+1}|\zeta\psi_{-,2n+2}\rangle\approx\int\frac{d\psi_{+,2n+1}^{*}d\psi_{+,2n+1}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\\
\times\frac{d\psi_{-,2n+1}^{*}d\psi_{-,2n+1}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\frac{d\psi_{+,2n}^{*}d\psi_{+,2n}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\frac{d\psi_{-,2n}^{*}d\psi_{-,2n}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\\
\times e^{\left(\psi_{+,2n+2}^{*}-\psi_{+,2n+1}^{*}\right)\psi_{+,2n+1}+\left(\psi_{+,2n+1}^{*}-\psi_{+,2n}^{*}\right)\psi_{+,2n}+\psi_{-,2n}^{*}\left(\psi_{-,2n+1}-\psi_{-,2n}\right)}\\
\times e^{\psi_{-,2n+1}^{*}\left(\psi_{-,2n+2}-\psi_{-,2n+1}\right)+\mathcal{L}_{+-,n}\Delta t}\langle\psi_{+,2n}|\hat{\rho}_{n}|\zeta\psi_{-,2n}\rangle.

(136)

Iterating this relation yields a discrete-time field integral representation of
the Keldysh partition function
Z(t)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\hat{\rho}(t))
,

Z(t)\approx\int\prod_{n=0}^{2N}\prod_{\sigma=\pm}\frac{d\psi_{\sigma,n}^{*}d\psi_{\sigma,n}}{\pi^{(1+\zeta)/2}}\,e^{iS}\langle\psi_{+,0}|\hat{\rho}(t_{0})|\zeta\psi_{-,0}\rangle,

(137)

where the Lindblad-Keldysh action is given by

S=\sum_{n=0}^{2N-1}\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left(-i\frac{\psi_{+,n+1}^{*}-\psi_{+,n}^{*}}{\Delta t/2}\psi_{+,n}-i\psi_{-,n}^{*}\frac{\psi_{-,n+1}-\psi_{-,n}}{\Delta t/2}\right)\\
-i\left(\psi_{-,2N}^{*}\psi_{+,2N}-\psi_{+,2N}^{*}\psi_{+,2N}-\psi_{-,2N}^{*}\psi_{-,2N}\right)-i\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Delta t\mathcal{L}_{+-,n}.

(138)

The first term in the second line follows from taking the trace to obtain the
Keldysh partition function
Z=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\hat{\rho}(t))=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\hat{\rho}_{N})
: Setting

n=N
in Eq. (
133
), and
denoting by
\{\lvert\alpha\rangle\}
a basis of Fock space, we find that

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\hat{\rho}_{N})
contains the factor

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(\lvert\psi_{+,2N}\rangle\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2N}\rvert\right)=\sum_{\alpha}\langle\alpha|\psi_{+,2N}\rangle\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2N}|\alpha\rangle\\
=\sum_{\alpha}\langle\psi_{-,2N}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|\psi_{+,2N}\rangle=\langle\psi_{-,2N}|\psi_{+,2N}\rangle=e^{\psi_{-,2N}^{*}\psi_{+,2N}},

(139)

where in the second equality we have exchanged the order of

\langle\alpha|\psi_{+,2N}\rangle
and
\langle\zeta\psi_{-,2N}|\alpha\rangle
,
leading to a sign change of
\psi_{-,2N}
for the case of Grassmann fields,
and in the third equality we have used

\hat{1}=\sum_{\alpha}\lvert\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha\rvert
. To take the continuum limit

N\to\infty
and thus
\Delta t\to 0
, we write

\psi_{n}=\psi(t_{n})\to\psi(t)
. In each of the matrix elements
H_{\pm,n}
,

L_{\pm,n}
, and
L^{\dagger}_{\pm,n}
defined in
Eq. (
134
), for
N\to\infty
we can treat the
fields as being evaluated at the same instant in time. Crucially, however,

L_{+,n}
is evaluated infinitesimally earlier than
L^{\dagger}_{+,n}
, which
can be accounted for by writing

L^{\dagger}_{+,n}L_{+,n}\to L^{\dagger}(t)L(t_{-})
where

t_{\pm}\to t\pm 0^{+}
. Similarly,
L_{-,n}
is evaluated infinitesimally
later than
L^{\dagger}_{-,n}
, thus

L^{\dagger}_{-,n}L_{-,n}\to L^{\dagger}_{-}(t)L_{-}(t_{+})
. Therefore, in the
continuum limit, the action in Eq. (
138
)
reproduces Eq. (
6
). The method of introducing additional
resolutions of the identity can be extended to subdivide products of operators
appearing in
\hat{H}
,
\hat{L}
, and
\hat{L}^{\dagger}
. This can be required
to accommodate antinormal order of jump operators
Huang et al. 2022
.

A.3
Temporal order and causality

We conclude this appendix by pointing out that the regularized representation of
the Lindbladian in the Keldysh action Eq. (
7
)
has the important property of obeying the causality structure of Keldysh
actions, while this is not the case if normal instead of temporal ordering of
the product
\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
is employed to construct the field
integral. As discussed in Sec.
II.2.2
, causality requires that

\mathcal{L}_{+-}=0
when
\psi_{+}=\psi_{-}
. Clearly, this is the case for
Eq. (
7
) when we ignore the infinitesimal time
shifts. Instead of introducing these time shifts through an additional time step
as described above, we could also have normal ordered the product

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
. However, as we illustrate in the following for the
example of a dephasing process, normal ordering leads to a Keldysh action that violates
causality in the continuous time limit.

Dephasing of a single particle is described by the jump operator

\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}
, where we consider
here bosonic creation and annihilation operators
\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}
and

\hat{\psi}
, respectively. The normal ordered form of the product

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
reads

\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}=\gamma\left(\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}\hat{\psi}+\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}\right)
.
Therefore, normal order of
\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
leads to the following
modified form
\mathcal{L}_{+-}^{\mathrm{no}}
of
\mathcal{L}_{+-}
given in
Eq. (
7
):

\mathcal{L}_{+-}^{\mathrm{no}}=\mathcal{L}_{+-}+\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}}{2}\left(L_{+}+L_{-}\right),

(140)

where
L_{\pm}=\psi^{*}_{\pm}\psi_{\pm}
. Clearly, the normal ordered version
does not obey the causality structure discussed in
Sec.
II.2.2
,
\mathcal{L}_{+-}^{\mathrm{no}}\neq 0
for

\psi_{+}=\psi_{-}
. We emphasize that for single-particle processes such as
single-particle loss with
\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\psi}
or particle gain
with
\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}
, the time ordering and
normal ordering of
\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L}
yield the same result (apart from
an unimportant constant) in the functional integral. However, for higher order
terms, such as two-body gain

\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}
or dephasing

\hat{L}=\sqrt{\gamma}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}
, only time ordering
yields a Keldysh action that obeys causality in the continuous time limit.

Appendix B
Green’s functions in the Keldysh formalism

In this appendix, we summarize several important properties of the retarded,
advanced, and Keldysh Green’s functions defined in
Eq. (
10
). We focus on open systems with time evolution
generated by a Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
.

B.1
From operators to fields

In the operator formalism of second quantization, the second equalities in each
of the lines of Eq. (
10
) define the retarded, advanced,
and Keldysh Green’s functions. Here, we show how to obtain the corresponding
expressions in Keldysh field theory. We consider first the retarded Green’s
function,

G^{R}(t,t^{\prime})=-i\theta(t-t^{\prime})\left(\langle\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\rangle-\zeta\langle\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\psi}(t)\rangle\right).

(141)

Assuming that time evolution is generated by a Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
, we
evaluate two-time averages using the quantum regression
theorem
Gardiner and Zoller 2014
. For the time order
t>t^{\prime}>t_{0}
imposed by the
Heaviside step function, we obtain

\langle\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(\hat{\psi}e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)}\left(\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t^{\prime}-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\right)\right)=\langle\psi_{+}(t)\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle,

(142)

where we have used the rule formulated at the end of
Sec.
II.2.1
to translate field operators
that multiply the density matrix from the left to fields on the forward
branch. Alternatively, using the cyclic property of the trace, we can write

\begin{split}\langle\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\rangle&=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)}\left(\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t^{\prime}-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\right)\hat{\psi}\right)\\
&=\zeta\langle\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{-}(t)\rangle=\langle\psi_{-}(t)\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle.\end{split}

(143)

Taking the sum and difference of the two alternative representations of the
two-time average, we obtain the relations

\langle\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle\psi_{c}(t)\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle,\quad 0=\langle\psi_{q}(t)\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle.

(144)

The latter equality is a manifestation of the causality structure of correlation
functions, discussed in full generality below, according to which any multi-time
expectation value vanishes when the largest time argument is associated with a
quantum field. Note that the sign
\zeta=-1
for Grassmann fields on the
backward branch in Eq. (
143
), which
results from the representation of the trace in fermionic Fock space in terms of
coherent states, ensures causality for fermions.

For the second two-time average in Eq. (
141
), again with

t>t^{\prime}>t_{0}
, the quantum regression theorem yields

\begin{split}\langle\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\psi}(t)\rangle&=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(\hat{\psi}e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)}\left[\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t^{\prime}-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\right)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\right]\right)\\
&=\zeta\langle\psi_{+}(t)\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{+}(t)\rangle,\end{split}

(145)

or, equivalently,

\langle\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\psi}(t)\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)}\left[\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t^{\prime}-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\right)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\right]\hat{\psi}\right)=\langle\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{-}(t)\rangle,

(146)

which can be combined to

\langle\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\psi}(t)\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{c}(t)\rangle,\quad 0=\langle\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{q}(t)\rangle.

(147)

Therefore, using Eqs. (
144
) and (
147
), the
retarded Green’s function Eq. (
141
) can be written as

\begin{split}G^{R}(t,t^{\prime})&=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\theta(t-t^{\prime})\left(\langle\psi_{c}(t)\psi_{+}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle-\zeta\langle\psi_{-}^{*}(t^{\prime})\psi_{c}(t)\rangle\right)\\
&=-i\theta(t-t^{\prime})\langle\psi_{c}(t)\psi_{q}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle=-i\langle\psi_{c}(t)\psi_{q}^{*}(t^{\prime})\rangle.\end{split}

(148)

In the last equality, we have used that according to
Eqs. (
144
) and (
147
), the two-time
expectation value vanishes when the quantum field is inserted at a later time
than the classical field, and, therefore, we can omit the Heaviside step
function. This proves the first line in Eq. (
10
). The
derivations for the advanced and Keldysh Green’s functions proceed analogously
after introducing a factor of
1=\theta(t-t^{\prime})+\theta(t^{\prime}-t)
to impose a
definite time order on each of the two-time averages appearing in the Keldysh
Green’s function.

B.2
Equal time arguments and Hermiticity

Expressing the Green’s functions as expectation values of operators is also
useful for deriving various exact properties. First, note that by setting

t^{\prime}=t\mp 0^{+}
in, respectively, the retarded and advanced Green’s functions
in Eq. (
10
), the Heaviside step functions evaluate to unity,
and the time arguments of the fields can be taken to be equal, which leads to

G^{R}(t,t-0^{+})=-G^{A}(t,t+0^{+})=-i.

(149)

Further, by using explicit representations of two-time averages as in
Eqs. (
142
) and (
145
), and
preservation of Hermiticity as expressed through the relation

\left(\mathcal{L}\hat{A}\right)^{\dagger}=\mathcal{L}\!\left(\hat{A}^{\dagger}\right)

for an arbitrary operator
\hat{A}
, it is straightforward to show that

G^{R}=\left(G^{A}\right)^{\dagger},\qquad G^{K}=-\left(G^{K}\right)^{\dagger}.

(150)

Hermitian conjugation of the Green’s functions amounts to complex conjugation,
interchanging the time arguments, and, for multicomponent fields, interchanging
the field indices.

B.3
Causality structure

As anticipated below Eq. (
144
), a key property of Green’s
functions that generalizes to multi-time correlation functions is what we refer
to as their causality structure,

\langle\psi_{q}(t)\cdots\rangle=\langle\psi_{q}^{*}(t)\cdots\rangle=0,

(151)

where “
\cdots
” stands for an arbitrary product of fields with time arguments
smaller than
t
. To derive this property, we consider two ordered sequences of
times,
t_{+,N_{+}}>t_{+,N_{+}-1}>\dotsb>t_{+,1}
and

t_{-,N_{-}}>t_{-,N_{-}-1}>\dotsb>t_{-,1}
, and initial and final times

t_{0}<t_{\pm,1}
and
t>t_{\pm,N_{\pm}}
, respectively. For operators

\hat{a}
,
\hat{b}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{b}_{N_{+}}
, and

\hat{c}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{c}_{N_{-}}
, that are each one of
\hat{\psi}
or

\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}
, we define a multi-time correlation function as

G(t,t_{+,1},\dotsc,t_{+,N_{+}},t_{-,1},\dotsc,t_{-,N_{-}})\\
=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(\hat{a}(t)\hat{b}_{N_{+}}(t_{+,N_{+}})\dotsb\hat{b}_{1}(t_{+,1})\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\hat{c}_{1}(t_{-,1})\dotsb\hat{c}_{N_{-}}(t_{-,N_{-}})\right).

(152)

The explicit form of this correlation function depends on the relative order of
the times
t_{\pm,n}
. For example, for
N_{+}=1
,
N_{-}=2
, and

t_{0}<t_{+,1}<t_{-,1}<t_{-,2}<t
, we obtain

G(t,t_{+,1},t_{-,1},t_{-,2})=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left(\hat{a}e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t-t_{-,2}\right)}\left\{\left[e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t_{-,2}-t_{-,1}\right)}\right.\right.\right.\\
\left.\left.\left.\times\left(\left\{e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t_{-,1}-t_{+,1}\right)}\left[\hat{b}_{1}\left(e^{\mathcal{L}\left(t_{+,1}-t_{0}\right)}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\right)\right]\right\}\hat{c}_{1}\right)\right]\hat{c}_{2}\right\}\right).

(153)

The pattern of inserting evolution superoperators
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
and
operators
\hat{b}_{n}
and
\hat{c}_{n}
on the left- and right-hand-side of the
density matrix generalizes to arbitrary relative time orders and numbers of
operators. A field integral representation of the multi-time correlation
function can be obtained as outlined in
Appendix
A
, whereby the operators

\hat{b}_{n}
and
\hat{c}_{n}
are replaced by fields on the forward and backward
branch,
b_{n,+}
and
c_{n,-}
, respectively. Due to the cyclic property of
the trace, the operator
\hat{a}
can equivalently be placed on the forward and
backward branches,

\langle a_{+}(t)\cdots\rangle=\zeta\langle\cdots a_{-}(t)\rangle=\langle a_{-}(t)\cdots\rangle
,
where in the last equality we have used that for the correlation function to be
nonzero for fermions, “
\cdots
” must correspond to a product of an odd number
of operators. We thus obtain
\langle a_{q}(t)\cdots\rangle=0
. Further, by
moving, e.g., one of the operators
\hat{c}_{n}(t_{-,n})
in
Eq. (
152
) from the right- to the
appropriate position on the left-hand-side, keeping the time order intact, we
obtain the same relation
\langle a_{q}(t)\cdots\rangle=0
but with

c_{n,-}(t_{-,n})
replaced by
c_{n,+}(t_{-,n})
. Generalizing this
argument, we see that the relation
\langle a_{q}(t)\cdots\rangle=0
holds for
any choice of branch indices of the fields representing the operators

\hat{b}_{n}
and
\hat{c}_{n}
, which in turn implies
Eq. (
151
).

Appendix C
Gaussian states and Gaussian actions

A particularly important subset of nonequilibrium systems is formed by Gaussian
evolution protocols. In the operator formulation, this corresponds to
Lindbladian evolution where at most two creation or annihilation operators act
simultaneously on the state
\hat{\rho}
;
see
Eisert and Prosen 2010
;
Prosen 2008
;
Prosen 2010
;
Barthel and Zhang 2022
for
treatments in the operator formalism. In the framework of Keldysh field theory,
this is equivalent to an action which is at most quadratic in bosonic or
fermionic fields. As such, technically, Gaussian dynamics can be solved formally
exactly and thus represents an important example for the equivalence of the
operator-based Lindbladian evolution and the Keldysh field integral. In the
following, we demonstrate this equivalence and provide a simple general
framework for the dynamics of Gaussian systems. Physically, their significance
lies in the fact that many interacting models can be reduced approximately to
Gaussian models, by means of suitable mean-field approaches (see, e.g.,
Sec.
XIII.1
).

Consider a bosonic (fermionic) system with
N
discrete single-particle degrees
of freedom, e.g., lattice sites, lattice momenta or atomic orbitals, which are
labeled with an index
\alpha=1,\dotsc,N
. The states in the many-body Hilbert
space are generated by the bosonic (fermionic) creation and annihilation
operators
\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}
and
\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}
, respectively, with

[\hat{\psi}_{\alpha},\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime}}]_{\zeta}=\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
.
It is convenient to work in a Nambu basis and collect annihilation and creation
operators in one Nambu vector

\hat{A}=(\hat{\psi}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{\psi}_{N},\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{1},\dotsc,\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{N})^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}
with
2N
entries.

When the particle number is not conserved, it is further convenient to work in a
basis of real bosons or Majorana fermions by applying the unitary transformation

\hat{u}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}+\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}),\hat{u}_{\alpha+N}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}-\hat{\psi}_{\alpha}^{\dagger})
.
It is equivalent to multiplying the Nambu vector with a unitary matrix
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\hat{u}=\Sigma\hat{A},\quad\Sigma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathds{1}_{N\times N}&\mathds{1}_{N\times N}\\
i\mathds{1}_{N\times N}&-i\mathds{1}_{N\times N}\end{array}\right).

Consider now a general Lindblad master equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}=-i\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right]+\sum_{n}\left(2\hat{L}_{n}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}^{\dagger}_{n}-\left\{\hat{L}^{\dagger}_{n}\hat{L}_{n},\hat{\rho}\right\}\right),

(156)

with linear jump operators
\hat{L}_{n}
and Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
. In the case of a Gaussian evolution, we can parameterize the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\hat{A}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}H_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\hat{A}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{A}^{\dagger}H\hat{A}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{u}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\tilde{H}\hat{u}

(157)

in terms of the complex
2N\times 2N
matrices
H
or

\tilde{H}=\Sigma H\Sigma^{\dagger}
. The boson and fermion exchange statistics
are encoded in
\tilde{H}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}=\zeta\tilde{H}
. Further, the Lindblad
operators can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{\alpha}l^{(n)}_{\alpha}\hat{A}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(l^{(n)})^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\hat{A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(l^{(n)})^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\Sigma^{\dagger}\hat{u}.

(158)

This yields a compact definition of the terms

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\hat{L}^{\dagger}_{n}\hat{L}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{A}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\hat{A}_{\beta}\left[\sum_{n}(l_{\alpha}^{(n)})^{*}l_{\beta}^{(n)}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\hat{A}^{\dagger}M\hat{A}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{u}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\tilde{M}\hat{u},

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\hat{L}_{n}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{n}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}\hat{A}_{\beta}\hat{\rho}\hat{A}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}M_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}\hat{u}_{\beta}\hat{\rho}\hat{u}_{\alpha}\tilde{M}_{\alpha\beta}.

(159)

Again,
\tilde{M}=\Sigma M\Sigma^{\dagger}
is defined via the unitary
transformation
\Sigma
. The matrices
M
and
\tilde{M}
are
Hermitian. Therefore, we can express them in terms of their real and imaginary
parts

M=D+iP,\quad 2D=M+M^{*},\quad 2P=-i(M-M^{*})

(160)

and analogously for
\tilde{M}
, which implies
D^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}=D
and
P^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}=-P
.

When the state
\hat{\rho}(t)
is Gaussian, all information is contained in the
single-particle covariance matrix that is equivalent to the equal-time Keldysh
Green’s function defined in Eq. (
10
),

\Gamma_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\!\left([\hat{A}_{\alpha},\hat{A}^{\dagger}_{\beta}]_{-\zeta}\hat{\rho}(t)\right)=iG^{K}_{\alpha,\beta}(t,t^{\prime}),

(161)

as also noted in Eq. (
29
) for a scalar bosonic field in a
spatial continuum. We denote the covariance matrix for real fields by

\gamma_{\alpha\beta}=\text{tr}([\hat{u}_{\alpha},\hat{u}_{\beta}]_{-\zeta}\hat{\rho}(t))
. Its equation of motion is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\gamma=i\left(\tilde{R}\gamma-\gamma\tilde{R}^{\dagger}\right)-\tilde{K},

(162)

with
R=H-iD,K=2P
for fermions and
R=\sigma(H+P),K=2\sigma^{\dagger}D\sigma,\sigma=i\sigma^{y}\otimes\mathds{1}_{N\times N}
for bosons. It has the
solution

\displaystyle\gamma(t)=e^{i\tilde{R}t}\gamma(0)e^{-i\tilde{R}^{\dagger}t}-\int_{-t}^{0}d\tau e^{-i\tilde{R}\tau}\tilde{K}e^{i\tilde{R}^{\dagger}\tau}.

(163)

In the long time limit, this yields the matrix equation

\displaystyle\gamma(t\to\infty)=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\omega-\tilde{R}}\tilde{K}\frac{1}{\omega-\tilde{R}^{\dagger}}.

(164)

With this preparation, we can readily write down the Keldysh field integral for
fermions and bosons. Following the conventional procedure, we define boson and
fermion fields on the
\pm
branches. For convenience, we will collect them in a

4N
-component vector
A=(A_{+},A_{-})^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}
with

A_{\pm}=(\psi_{1,\pm},\dotsc,\psi_{N,\pm},\psi_{1,\pm}^{*},\dotsc,\psi_{N,\pm}^{*})

and
\psi_{\alpha,\pm}^{*}
denoting the conjugate field (complex conjugate for
bosons, independent Grassmann field for fermions). This leads to an action

\displaystyle S=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,A^{*{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\mathcal{G}_{\pm}A,

(165)

with the
4N\times 4N
matrix in the
\pm
-basis (
\Sigma_{z}=\sigma_{z}\otimes\mathds{1}_{N\times N}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\pm}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\Sigma_{z}\partial_{t}-H+iM&-2iM^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\intercal$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\intercal$}}}}\\
0_{2N\times 2N}&-i\Sigma_{z}\partial_{t}+H+iM\end{array}\right).

For bosons, one can readily perform the transformation to real fields, yielding

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\pm}^{(b)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\sigma\omega-\tilde{H}+i\tilde{D}&-i\tilde{D}-\tilde{P}\\
-i\tilde{D}+\tilde{P}&-i\sigma\omega+\tilde{H}+i\tilde{D}\end{array}\right),

where we have exploited the symmetrization rules of
\tilde{M}
and the boson
fields. Performing the conventional boson Keldysh rotation, we obtain the
inverse Green’s function

\begin{split}G^{-1}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\sigma\omega-\tilde{H}-\tilde{P}\\
i\sigma\omega-\tilde{H}+\tilde{P}&-2i\tilde{D}\end{array}\right)\\
&=i\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sigma(\omega-\tilde{R})\\
(\omega-\tilde{R}^{\dagger})\sigma&-\sigma\tilde{K}\sigma\end{array}\right),\end{split}

(170)

which when inverted yields the result in Eq. (
164
) for bosons. The elements of the inverted matrix are the Green’s functions

\displaystyle G=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}G^{K}&G^{R}\\
G^{A}&0\end{array}\right)

as given in the main text in Eq. (
10
).

For fermions, exploiting the anticommutation rules of the Majorana fields yields a Keldysh Green’s function

\displaystyle G^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\omega-\tilde{H}-i\tilde{D}\\
\omega-\tilde{H}+i\tilde{D}&2\tilde{P}\end{array}\right),

which again yields the result in Eq. (
164
) but for fermions.

Appendix D
Dark states of Lindblad dynamics

Dark states are pure-state dynamical fixed points of Lindbladians. We consider a
driven open quantum system evolving according to the quantum master equation
(see also Eq. (
1
))

\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}\,=-i\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right]+\sum^{M}_{l=1}\gamma_{l}\left(2\hat{L}_{l}^{\,}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{l}^{\dagger}-\left\{\hat{L}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{l}^{\,},\hat{\rho}\right\}\right).

(175)

A
dark state

\lvert D\rangle
, with associated pure state density matrix

\hat{\rho}_{D}=\lvert D\rangle\langle D\rvert
, is defined by the two conditions:

\hat{H}\lvert D\rangle=E\lvert D\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{L}_{l}\lvert D\rangle=0\quad\forall l.

(176)

Dark states are thus zero modes of the Lindbladian, or in more physical terms,
fixed points of Lindblad dynamics,
\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}_{D}=0
, with the
additional property of being pure. They are called ‘dark’ since they are not
affected by dissipation: for instance, in a quantum optics setup, a system in a
dark state is decoupled from the radiation field and will not emit any photons.

For the case
\hat{H}=0
, the following two requirements have to be fulfilled
to ensure uniqueness of the dark state as an attractor of dynamics: (i) The dark
subspace is one-dimensional, i.e., there is exactly one normalized dark state
which fulfills Eq. (
176
). (ii) No stationary solutions other than
the dark state exist. A sufficient criterion for this situation to occur is
provided in Ref.
Kraus et al. 2008
.

Uniqueness is a key property, since, under this circumstance, the system will be
attracted toward the dark state for an arbitrary initial density matrix. That
is, starting from any
\hat{\rho}(t_{0})
, the dynamics will purify the density
matrix:

\hat{\rho}(t_{0})=\sum_{n}p_{n}\lvert\psi_{n}\rangle\langle\psi_{n}\rvert\overset{t\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\lvert D\rangle\langle D\rvert.

(177)

In the case of a dark subspace (e.g., in the context of topological systems,
cf. Sec.
XIII.2
) that is spanned by several linearly independent
states, the dynamics will still be attracted to this subspace as long as
condition (ii) above is satisfied.

Comprehensive overviews of the concept of dark states in many-body systems and
their applications to modern quantum simulators can be found in
Ref.
Müller et al. 2012
;
Harrington et al. 2022
.
